通膨升溫壓抑美股表現，近期聚焦財報結果
2026 年 10 月 15 日
一、 市場動態
1. 科技股領跌，費半連 6 日收黑：美股主要指數 10 月 14 日全數下跌，道瓊工業指數下
跌 0.09%，收 51,621.48 點；S&P 500 指數下跌 1.11%，收 7,718.94 點，為 7 月 29
日以來最大單日跌幅；Nasdaq 綜合指數下跌 1.88%，收 26,692.87 點，為 7 月 23
日以來最大單日跌幅。費城半導體指數下跌 4.30%，收 11,684.38 點，連續第６個交
易日走低，為 2025 年 5 月以來最長連跌紀錄。

2. 類股非全面下挫，資金仍呈輪動現象：半導體及 AI 相關股票在 10/14 跌幅尤其明顯
，AMD 下跌 5.73%、Nvidia 下跌 3.16%、Broadcom 下跌 3.10%，AI 基礎建設相關
的 CoreWeave、Nebius、Oracle 及 Dell 跌幅更深，顯示市場對資本支出規模較大、
融資成本敏感度較高的 AI 相關企業風險偏好明顯下降。不過，S&P 500 的 11 大類
股中有 8 類下跌，資訊科技及通訊服務為主要拖累來源，但醫療保健、必需消費與能
源類股仍逆勢上漲。
3. 美國 10 年期公債殖利率盤中急升後回落：美國 10 年期公債殖利率 10/14 由 5.2%左
右快速升至約 5.33%，逼近近期高點；然而，隨後市場消化 Fed 官員較審慎的政策訊
號，殖利率自高檔回落，終場約收於 5.19%，較前一交易日 5.21%小幅下降約 2 個基
點。
二、 下跌原因分析
1. 美 9 月通膨高於預期 ，Fed 再升息風險重新升高 ：美國 10/14 公布 9 月 CPI 月增 0.6%

、年增率也由 8 月的 3.4%上揚至 3.7%；核心 CPI 月增 0.3%、年增率由 8 月的 2.4%
升至 2.6%。其中汽油價格單月大漲 9.1%，貢獻整體 CPI 月增幅超過一半；住宅成本
亦月增 0.4%，顯示物價壓力仍具黏性。此數據公布後，市場重新提高 Fed 進一步升
息的預期 ，CME FedWatch 所反映的 10 月升息 1 碼機率一度由前一日約 22%提高至
37%。雖然截至 10/15 台北時間早上 7 點左右該機率回降至 29.3%，這也提醒投資人
Fed 的升息路徑仍存在不確定性。
2. 高利率同時壓縮科技股估值與提高 AI 融資成本 ：科技及半導體公司未來獲利成長占評
價的比重較高，因此對折現率變化相對敏感。當長天期美債殖利率維持 5%以上，市
場願意支付的本益比自然受到限制，即使企業獲利仍維持成長，股價也可能因評價倍
數下降而出現修正。另一方面，本輪 AI 投資需要大量資料中心、伺服器、電力與網路
基礎設施，高利率亦直接增加企業融資成本，反映信用市場對高度依賴外部資金的 AI

基礎建設公司要求更高風險溢酬。

策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
3. AI 投資焦點由「支出規模」轉向「投資報酬率」:近期市場對 AI 的疑慮逐漸由「需求
是否成長」 ，轉向「龐大的資本支出能否轉化為足夠營收、獲利與自由現金流」 。
Broadcom 執行長陳福陽近期亦指出 ，AI 未來發展的重要限制可能逐漸由晶片或記憶
體供應轉向資本本身。我們認為，這並不代表AI 資本支出週期即將結束，而是市場已
由第一階段的 「資本支出愈大、成長想像愈高」 ，逐步進入更重視資本效率與投資報酬
率的第二階段。未來財務結構、現金流與AI 商業化能力，將成為不同企業評價分化的
重要因素。
4. 中國記憶體競爭疑慮加重半導體短線壓力：中國長鑫存儲（CXMT）10/10 傳出記憶

體技術取得進展 ， 引發市場對未來DRAM 供給增加及價格競爭的疑慮 ， 記憶體相關類
股率先承壓，並進一步影響整體半導體市場情緒。惟 DRAM 與 GPU、客製化 ASIC、
先進晶圓製程及先進封裝的產業競爭與需求結構並不相同。我們認為，儘管記憶體競
爭升溫值得關注，但目前尚不足以延伸為整體 AI 半導體需求同步轉弱。
5. 高檔籌碼加速獲利了結：AI 與半導體仍是 2026 年美股表現最強勢的投資主題之一，
部分科技股今年累計漲幅已相當可觀。當通膨、殖利率及AI 投資報酬疑慮同時升高，
先前漲幅較大、投資人部位較集中的族群自然成為優先調節對象。因此，本波費半快
速修正除了總經與基本面疑慮外，亦包含籌碼降溫及風險部位收縮因素，使短期價格
波動幅度可能高於企業基本面實際變化。
三、市場看法
1. 類股輪動明顯，尚非全面看空美股：科技與 AI 相關股票 10 月 14 日明顯承壓，但醫
療保健、必需消費與能源類股仍逆勢上漲，道瓊工業指數僅下跌 0.09%。此外，S&P
500 成分股下跌家數與上漲家數之比約為 1.1：1；相較於指數下跌 1.11%，市場廣度

並未同步大幅惡化。這反映資金主要由高評價且前期漲幅較大的科技與AI 族群，轉向
現金流較穩定的防禦型產業。現階段市場特徵較接近類股輪動與風格切換，而非無差
別的全面風險撤離，亦尚未看到美股企業獲利或經濟基本面全面反轉的明確訊號。
2. 美股科技股財報前風險降載，Q3 仍存在重新定價空間：近期科技股修正，費城半導
體指數連續６個交易日下跌，也可能部分反映第三季財報季正式展開前，投資人在股
價先前漲幅較大、評價偏高及利率環境不確定下，提前降低風險曝險。真正決定後續
市場方向的關鍵 ， 仍在於實際企業獲利能否支持目前的高成長預期 。根據 Bloomberg
季度獲利成長追蹤資料，今年 Q1 與 Q2 財報季期間的 EPS 成長率經驗顯示，隨實際
財報陸續公布，企業獲利成長曾明顯優於財報季開始前的市場預期。儘管這並不保證
Q3 將複製前兩季的上修幅度，但目前市場因財報不確定性所反映的風險折價仍存在
被實際財報重新驗證的可能。若大型科技及半導體公司後續營收、獲利與AI 需求指引
實際公布結果優於預期，近期風險溢價可望部分收斂；反之，若獲利成長無法跟上目

前評價，市場整理時間則可能進一步延長。

不另行通知。
3. 費半修正幅度大，但 AI 需求尚未出現全面反轉：ASML 在 10/14 所公布的最新第三
季財報反而顯示 AI 相關設備需求仍相當強勁，該公司表示 2027 與 2028 年規劃產能
可能仍無法完全滿足客戶需求，部分客戶亦已表達對 2029 及 2030 年設備需求的興
趣。儘管費城半導體指數近期市場情緒轉弱，但由 ASML 的財報表現來看，目前仍未
看到大型 AI 客戶全面取消晶片訂單、主要雲端服務商同步削減 AI 資本支出，或先進
製程需求快速降溫等結構性訊號。
4. 台積電營收顯示 AI 需求暢旺 ， 留意10/15 法說會內容：台積電 10/8 公布的第三季營

收達新台幣 1.49 兆元、年增約 50%，創歷史新高，並高於市場原先預估的 1.46 兆元
；9 月單月營收年增 54.6%。強勁的營收表現仍反映 AI 與先進製程需求具有支撐。目
前市場因此呈現「企業營收仍快速成長，但股票評價下降」的狀況。高殖利率環境下
，EPS 上升與本益比下降可以同時發生，後續股價方向將取決於企業獲利上修幅度是
否足以抵銷評價收縮。該公司將於 10/15 下午舉行第三季法人說明會。市場重點將集
中於第四季展望、AI 與 HPC 需求、先進製程、先進封裝及未來資本支出規劃。公司
對 AI 需求與產能投資的最新判斷，將成為判斷近期費半修正主要屬於評價調整，或產
業基本面是否開始出現變化的重要依據。
5. AI 與半導體進入基本面分化階段：我們認為，在高利率環境下，高度仰賴外部融資、
自由現金流尚未成熟的 AI 基礎建設業者 ， 受到的資金成本壓力明顯高於具穩定現金流
的大型科技與雲端服務商。同樣地，記憶體、GPU、ASIC、先進製程及先進封裝各有
不同供需結構。我們預期下一階段 AI 行情將更加重視訂單能見度、產品競爭力、毛利

率、自由現金流及 AI 營收實際兌現，產業內部強弱分化程度可能進一步提高。
6. Fed 路徑仍存不確定性，惟企業獲利可望提供股市支撐：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使市場重
新評估 Fed 後續升息時點與幅度，美國利率政策仍存在不確定性，長天期美債殖利率
也可能維持高檔。不過，現階段企業獲利成長與AI 相關需求仍具韌性，若第三季財報

不另行通知。
延續前兩季優於預期的趨勢，企業 EPS 若能上修有望部分抵銷高利率對本益比的壓縮
效果。換言之，後續市場表現未必只由 Fed 政策主導，企業實際獲利能否持續兌現，
將是支撐科技與半導體股中期走勢的重要力量。
四、投資建議
1. 短線聚焦美股財報與 Fed 決策，科技股中期趨勢料不致有變：近期市場同時面臨財報
前的觀望態勢、美債殖利率偏高及 AI 資本支出疑慮等不確定因素，短線風險偏好可能
受到壓抑，後續宜關注 10 月美股財報對企業獲利與資本支出展望，以及 FOMC 會議
所釋出的政策訊號。中期而言，目前AI 相關企業營運與資本支出尚未出現全面轉弱跡

象，科技產業基本面仍具一定支撐，但在利率維持高檔及市場評價偏高的環境下，後
續宜更加重視企業獲利、現金流及資本支出的實際變化。
2. 中天期債券風險報酬仍較均衡 ：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使短端殖利率持續受到 Fed 政策路
徑影響，而 20～30 年超長端仍面臨財政赤字、國債供給與期限溢價偏高等結構性壓
力。相較之下，目前殖利率已位於歷史相對高檔，若後續核心通膨或能源價格改善，
中段殖利率具有回落條件，同時其存續期間風險又低於超長端，因此現階段以中天期
美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構相對較佳。

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 美國 9 月 ISM 非製造業 PMI 從 8 月的 55.4 降至 54.9，顯示服務業活動增速放
緩，然而國內需求強勁使供應鏈承壓，並推動投入價格指數由 72.6 升至 74.0 為
四年多來新高；另一方面，美國 10 月密西根大學消費者信心指數由 9 月的 48.1
降至 46.3，為連續第三個月下降且創 5 月以來最低 ， 惟未來一年通膨預期由 4.6%
升至 4.7%，顯示通膨可能持續維持高位至 2027 年。
⚫ 聯準會 9 月 FOMC 會議紀要顯示與會官員均支持升息，並認為年底前尚需升息
一次 ，但對於後續升息時點及升息理由上存在分歧； 其中部分官員認為升息是防
止能源及其他價格衝擊造成更廣泛影響 的預防性措施，但更為鷹派官員則認為
升息在於抑制投資和支出，以防範正在顯現的需求驅動型通膨。
⚫ 歐洲央行 9 月貨幣政策會議紀要顯示全體決策委員均同意升息，並認為當前經
濟成長風險偏向下行 ， 通膨風險則偏向上行 ，因此將保留政策彈性， 根據經濟數

據做為決策，不提供未來利率路徑指引。
⚫ 台灣 9 月 CPI 年增率自 8 月 2.05%上升至 2.73%，已連續 5 個月超過 2%通膨警
戒線 ， 且創兩年半來最大漲幅 ，除了豪雨導致蔬果價格上漲及中秋假期的短期因
素外，油價居高不下亦使通膨壓力增加。
⚫ 中國央行發布匯率政策立場聲明， 強調中國貿易發展根源於產業國際競爭力的
提升，無必要通過匯率貶值獲取貿易競爭優勢 ，並指出中國長期實施以市場供需
為基礎、參考一籃子貨幣進行調節管理浮動匯率制度。

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Oct/12/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.699，低點在 31.940，以 31.875 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 4.5 分。 
上週台幣震盪走貶，週初，受到前週公布的美國非農就業數據低於預期，降低
市場對聯準會 10 月升息的預期， 台股創高吸引外資匯入 ， 推升台幣最高來到31.699；
不過隨著美伊緊張情勢升溫且台灣連假將至 ， 台股收低使外資轉為大幅賣超並匯出 ，
推低台幣回到 9 月中以來的震盪區間下緣，最低觸及 31.940，最終在央行調節之下
收在 31.875。 
＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國 10 月密西根大學消費者信心
指數持續下滑，由 9 月的 48.1 降至 46.3，創 5 月以來最低，惟未來一年通膨預期
升至 4.7%，加上中東局勢反覆，油價高位震盪，仍支撐市場對於聯準會年內升息
的預期，預計美元指數維持在 102 附近震盪，對台幣帶來壓力；就台灣市場來看，
台灣 9 月出口創歷史新高，年增 60.9%，反映 AI 及科技需求持續強勁，出口商後
續拋匯需求可望提供台幣支撐；然而，台股逼近五萬點關卡之際，外資未能延續買
超走勢，且連假前已大幅賣超台股，若後續外資持續匯出，將限制台幣升值空間。
綜合以上，預估本週台幣區間震盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.700-32.000

    預估本週人民幣區間偏升。就國際市場來看，美國密西根大學消費者信心指數
由 9 月的 48.1 降至 46.3，創5 月以來新低 ， 不過對於未來一年的通膨預期升至4.7%，
加上油價維持高檔 ， 市場對於聯準會年內升息的預期仍存 ， 美元指數維持高檔震盪 ，
將限制人民幣升值速度；就中國國內市場來看，人行中間價維持偏強設定，不過人
行表示將防範短期大幅波動與急貶風險 ， 並無意透過人民幣貶值取得貿易競爭優勢 ，
有助於穩定市場對人民幣的預期 ；最後就技術面來看 ，USD/CNH 日線圖中 ， 各天期
均線仍向下發散，空頭格局不變，下方支撐先看向 9 月底低點 6.6908。綜合以上，
預估本週人民幣區間偏升。

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週 USD/JPY 震盪走升。週初，因美元指數在102 附近盤整，帶領USD/JPY 延
續前一週走勢震盪走升到 158 上方；週間，因缺乏重要經濟數據，市場偏向觀望，
USD/JPY 維持在 158 附近震盪；臨近週末，USD/JPY 曾一度回測 157.50 附近，不
過在美日利差的主導下，USD/JPY 依舊保持偏多的走勢，終場收在 158.31，週線上
漲 84 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、路透短觀調查顯示，受半導
體強勁訂單推動，日本 10 月大型製造業信心升至正 22，創近五年新高；但原料成
本高漲重擊非製造業，信心指數降至正 23。與此同時，此前投下升息反對票的日銀
鴿派委員佐藤綾野立場軟化，表態支持分階段升息，市場對後續正常化預期升溫，
預計將限縮 USD/JPY 的上行空間 。二 、 日本首相高市早苗表示密切關注匯率與物價
走勢 ， 強調日本已脫離通縮 ， 將完全尊重央行政策獨立性 ， 暗示不反對進一步升息 。
面對通膨加劇與日圓走貶壓力，其過往堅持的貨幣寬鬆立場已出現轉向。然而目前
美日實質利差仍大，持續為 USD/JPY 提供支撐。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，
USD/JPY 持續沿著上彎的月線上攻，然匯價持續承壓於季線下方，在有效站上季線
及突破前高壓力前，仍以區間震盪看待。綜合以上，預估USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震
盪的可能性較高。

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 震盪 走跌 。週初， 因法國債券風險與區域政治不確定性 ，
EUR/USD 下行最低來到 1.1160； 週間 ，受中東地緣風險升溫與歐洲財政疑慮帶動避
險需求 ， 加上美債殖利率維持高檔 ， 美元利差優勢延續，歐元維持低檔； 臨近週末 ，
逢低歐元有買盤支撐，帶動歐元收復部分跌幅，終場收在 1.1201，週線下跌 46 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間偏空震盪，主要原因如下：一、歐洲央行首席經濟學
家連恩表示，夏末能源成本飆升為經濟增長帶來下行風險，並可能限制央行收緊貨
幣政策的力道，與此同時，歐元區 10 月 Sentix 投資者信心指數由 5.1 降至 2.7，德
國預期指數同步滑落，反映市場對經濟復甦的信心明顯降溫，預計將令歐元持續承
壓。二、法國社會動盪與支出訴求阻礙財政減赤進程，加劇債務可持續性疑慮，並
推升法德公債利差與信用風險，令歐元承壓走弱。相較美國經濟展現韌性且聯準會
維持偏鷹立場，歐洲深陷景氣放緩與財政掣肘，歐洲央行緊縮空間受限。在歐美基
本面與政策前景持續分歧下 ，EUR/USD 缺乏實質支撐 ， 後市將維持偏弱整理並下探
關鍵支撐。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價雖多次向下測試 1.1165 關
鍵價位均獲得支撐，惟每次反彈均無法站上五日線，且各天期均線下彎呈現空頭排
列。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間偏空震盪的可能性較高。

Report source: 西南证券
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

请务必阅读正文后的重要声明部分  
1 
[Table_ReportInfo] 2026 年 10 月 14 日      
证 券研究报告 •宏观简评报告      
数据点评  
生产与消费 价格修复分化 ，内需 回暖 尚有 “时间差 ” 
——2026 年 9 月通胀数据点评

点评

西 南证券研究院  
[Table_Summary]  摘要：CPI方面，9 月 CPI 同比上涨 0.9%，涨幅较上月扩大0.1个百分点，连
续两个月回升，但低于市场预期。食品价格同比降幅扩大至1.7%，主要受鲜
菜、 鲜果价格下跌影响， 猪价同比跌幅收窄； 非食品价格同比涨幅扩大至1.5%，
主要受国际油价反弹及电子产品价格上涨带动，其中汽油价格同比上涨
16.6%，拉动整体 CPI同比上涨约 0.47 个百分点。环比看，CPI 上涨 0.2%，
涨幅较上月收窄 0.2 个百分点，核心 CPI 同比涨幅持平于 1.0%，服务价格环
比转跌，反映国内终端需求修复仍偏温和。展望10 月，国际油价高位运行及
AI 算力需求带动的消费电子涨价仍将提供支撑， 但蔬果价格同比降幅扩大、 猪
肉价格环比转跌，叠加服务价格季节性回落，CPI 整体或延续温和修复。PPI
方面，9 月 PPI 同比上涨 5.0%， 涨幅较上月扩大1.2个百分点， 高于市场预期，
主要受国际油价反弹及低基数效应推动。分行业看，煤炭开采和洗选业、 石油
和天然气开采业、石油煤炭及其他燃料加工业价格同比涨幅分别扩大至
35.3%、24.5%和 21.6%，化工、化纤等行业价格亦明显回升，反映能源价格
上涨正沿产业链向中游原材料环节传导；电气机械、 计算机通信及其他电子设
备制造业价格环比均上涨0.4%，AI 算力相关需求形成一定支撑。 环比看，PPI
上涨 1.2%，涨幅较上月扩大 0.8 个百分点，石油相关行业价格上涨是主要拉
动。展望后续，美伊围绕霍尔木兹海峡的紧张局势、国际油价高位运行、冬季
煤炭备货及低基数效应仍将支撑PPI， 但上下游价格传导不畅、 终端消费偏弱，
生活资料价格上涨空间仍然有限，PPI 修复或延续结构性分化。 
 输入性因素支撑 CPI 同比修复，核心 CPI 保持稳定。同比来看，9 月 CPI 同
比涨幅扩大 0.1个百分点至 0.9%，连续两个月回升，增速低于wind一致预期
（1.02%）。其中，食品价格同比下降1.7%，降幅扩大 0.3个百分点。非食品
价格同比涨幅扩大 0.3 个百分点至 1.5%，主要受能源及电子产品价格涨幅扩
大影响。9 月中东地缘冲突持续发酵， 能源供应风险上升， 国际油价二次反弹，
带动国内能源价格涨幅扩大，对整体CPI 形成支撑。环比来看，9 月 CPI环比
涨幅收窄 0.2个百分点至 0.2%， 其中食品价格环比上升 0.4%，持平上月表现，
非食品价格环比涨幅收窄 0.1 个百分点至 0.2%。9 月核心 CPI 同比涨幅持平
于 1%， 核心CPI 环比回落至 0%。核心 CPI 连续数月运行在 1.0%附近， 说明
剔除输入性扰动后，国内终端需求的修复力度仍然温和。展望 10 月，国际油
价高位运行及 AI算力需求带动的消费电子价格上涨仍将为CPI提供支撑，但
食品价格仍面临一定下行压力。 同时， 暑期结束后服务价格进入季节性回落阶
段，核心 CPI 修复力度预计仍偏温和。整体来看，输入性因素仍将对CPI 形
成支撑，但内需修复偏缓或限制涨幅，预计10 月 CPI 同比延续温和回升。 
 食品价格同比降幅扩大，蔬果价格下跌是主要拖累。同比来看，9 月食品价格
同比下降 1.7%，降幅扩大 0.3 个百分点，主要受鲜菜、鲜果价格下跌及猪肉
价格仍处低位影响。 食品细项中，鲜菜、鲜果价格同比分别下降8.1%、1.5%，
降幅均扩大，反映季节性供给增加对农产品价格形成压制。牛肉、 羊肉价格同
比分别上升 7.5%、6.4%，涨幅均扩大。猪价同比下降 10.0%，降幅收窄 1.8
个百分点，价格弱势有所缓解，但同比仍处于较大跌幅区间。环比来看，食品
价格环比上升 0.4%，持平上月表现。食品细项中，鲜菜价格环比涨幅收窄5
个百分点至 0.5%，鲜果价格环比由下降转为上升 0.6%。猪价环比涨幅收窄
0.1 个百分点至 1.2%，9 月猪肉均价同比下降 13.58%，降幅继续收窄。从最
新的高频数据走势看，截至 2026年 10 月 13 日，同比方面，10 月初 28 种重
点监测蔬菜价格同比下降 17.1%，7 种重点监测水果价格同比下降 6.1%，同

2
比降幅均扩大。鸡蛋价格同比涨幅收窄至31.7%，猪肉价格同比下降 11.5%，
降幅收窄；环比方面，28 种重点监测蔬菜价格环比由上升转为下降5.9%，7
种重点监测水果价格环比下降 0.3%，降幅收窄。鸡蛋价格环比由上升转为下
降 9.6%，猪肉价格环比由上升转为下降2.2%。结合 10 月初高频数据，蔬果
价格同比降幅进一步扩大， 猪肉价格环比转跌， 食品价格短期仍面临一定下行
压力，猪肉价格修复尚不稳定，后续仍需观察生猪出栏节奏、养殖端产能调整
及消费需求变化。
 能源和电子产品支撑非食品价格， 暑期结束拖累服务价格环比表现。同比来看，
9 月非食品价格同比涨幅扩大0.3个百分点至 1.5%，主要受国际油价回升带动
能源价格上涨影响。9 月汽油价格同比涨幅扩大 7.3 个百分点至 16.6%，仅此
一项即影响 CPI同比上涨约 0.47 个百分点， 带动整体能源价格涨幅从4.1%跃
升至 7.3%。扣除能源的工业消费品价格上涨1.6%，涨幅回落 0.2 个百分点。

其中黄金饰品价格上涨 22.5%，涨幅回落 11.1 个百分点；数据存储设备、平
板电脑、 计算机和移动电话机价格分别上涨46.7%、23.6%、21.8%和 14.9%，
涨幅均有扩大，反映电子产品价格仍有较强支撑。服务价格同比上升 0.8%，
涨幅持平上月。环比来看，9 月非食品价格环比涨幅收窄0.1个百分点至 0.2%。
工业消费品价格上涨 0.7%，涨幅比上月回落 0.1 个百分点，其中国内汽油价
格上涨 4.9%，涨幅比上月回落2.3 个百分点， 黄金饰品价格由上月上涨7.6%
转为下降 2.1%。服务 CPI 环比由上升转为下降 0.2%。服务中， 随着暑期结束，
飞机票、 宾馆住宿和旅行社收费价格分别下降8.7%、7.0%和 6.0%； 受成本走
高影响，移动电话机、计算机、数据存储设备和平板电脑价格分别上涨 3.7%、
2.0%、1.7%和 1.5%。展望 10 月，非食品价格预计延续分化走势，同比仍受
到能源价格回升和电子产品涨价支撑， 但环比上涨动能可能进一步减弱。 一方

面， 国际油价同比改善以及电子产品价格上涨仍将支撑非食品价格表现； 另一
方面， 暑期结束后服务价格进入季节性回落阶段， 叠加黄金等前期涨价品类涨
幅收窄，非食品价格环比或继续放缓。
 油价反弹带动 PPI超预期回升，资源品价格韧性较强。从同比看，受国际油价
涨幅回升及低基数效应影响，PPI同比涨幅扩大 1.2 个百分点至 5.0%，增速高
于 wind 一致预期 （4.3%）。生产资料同比涨幅扩大1.5 个百分点至 6.5%，生
活资料同比下降 0.1%，降幅收窄 0.4 个百分点。分行业看，煤炭开采和洗选
业同比涨幅扩大 8.7个百分点至 35.3%，主要受冬季供暖备煤需求增加、 主产
矿区供应阶段性偏紧、国际煤价上涨预期效应等因素影响。石油和天然气开采
业、石油煤炭及其他燃料加工业价格同比涨幅分别扩大14.0、10.5 个百分点
至 24.5%和 21.6%。化学纤维制造业、 化学原料及化学制品制造业同比涨幅分
别扩大至 12.3%和 12.1%。一方面， 国际原油价格上涨直接推升采掘和炼化环

节价格；另一方面，石油作为基础原材料，通过燃料、化工品等渠道向中下游
传导， 带动化学原料、 化纤等行业价格上涨。从环比看，PPI环比涨幅扩大 0.8
个百分点至 1.2%， 生产资料环比涨幅扩大 1 个百分点至 1.4%， 生活资料环比
涨幅收窄 0.1 个百分点至 0.1%。分行业看，煤炭开采和洗选业环比涨幅扩大
6.7 个百分点至 9.5%；石油相关行业价格上涨动能显著增强。 石油和天然气开
采业、 石油煤炭及其他燃料加工业价格环比涨幅分别扩大4.3、4.9个百分点至
10.4%和 8.4%。电气机械和器材制造业、计算机通信和其他电子设备制造业
价格环比均上涨 0.4%，AI 与算电协同带来的结构性需求推动资本开支扩张。
国际层面，10 月 13 日布伦特原油现货价格为130.92美元/桶，10 月初油价同
比涨幅扩大 28.36 个百分点至 100.08%。10 月以来，美伊围绕霍尔木兹海峡
的紧张局势持续升温，伊朗加强对海上运输活动限制，美国持续强化对伊朗石
油贸易的封锁，区域供应风险上升。原油市场供给担忧升温，国际油价或维持

高位，并通过能源、化工等产业链向国内工业品价格传导。9 月，PPI 同比涨
幅（5.0%）与 CPI 同比涨幅（0.9%）之间的差距达到 4.1 个百分点，工厂端
的原材料和能源成本正在快速上涨，但尚未同步传导至终端消费市场，中下游
企业的利润空间正受到挤压。后续来看，国际油价高位震荡、冬季煤炭备货叠
加低基数构成后续 PPI 的核心推升力量。石油与煤炭开采价格同比或继续走
阔，并向化工、化纤等中下游传导；AI 算力相关电气设备、通信设备制造价
格或维持增长。 但上下游传导仍不畅， 终端消费偏弱或仍制约生活资料涨价空
间。
 风险提示：国内政策落地节奏不及预期，国际大宗商品价格波动超预期， 海外
地缘扰动风险。

分析师承诺  
本报告署名分析师具有中国证券业协会授予的证券投资咨询执业资格并注册为证券分析师，报告所采用的数据均
来自合法合规 渠道，分析逻辑基于分析师的职业理解，通过合理判断得出结论，独立、客观地出具本报告。分析师承
诺不曾因，不因，也将不会因本报告中的具体推荐意见或观点而直接或间接获取任何形式的补偿。

投资评级说明  
报告中投资建议所涉及的评级分为公司评级和行业评级（另有说明的除外）。评级标准为报告发布日后 6 个月内
的相对市场表现，即：以报告发布日后 6 个月内公司股价（或行业指数）相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数的涨跌幅
作为基准。其中：A 股市场以沪深 300 指数为基准， 新三板市场以三板成指 （针对协议转让标的） 或三板做市指数 （针
对做市转让标的）为基准；香港市场以恒生指数为基准；美国市场以纳斯达克综合指数或标普 500 指数为基准。  
公司评级  
买入：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在 20%以上  
持有：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 10%与 20%之间 
中性：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 -10%与 10%之间  
回避：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 -20%与-10%之间  
卖出：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在 -20%以下  
行业评级  
强于大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报高于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 5%以上 
跟随大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报介于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 -5%与 5%之间 
弱于大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报低于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 -5%以下

TSMC will host its 3Q26 analyst meeting on 15 October. We expect a 
strong finish to 2026 with a sustained growth outlook into 2027 for 
TSMC as demand broadens across AI GPUs/AI ASICs, networking 
and server CPUs. In particular, the stronger CPU demand driven by 
agentic AI represents a key change over the past year. We forecast 
2026E/2027E revenue growth of 42.0%/36.9% YoY (USD terms), 
with GM at 67.2%/67.5%. We also raise our 2027E/2028E CapEx 
forecasts to US$85bn/US$98bn (vs. US$78bn/US$82bn 
previously) as we expect TSMC to further ramp up its capacity 
investment to support longer-term customer demand. 
Strong sequential growth into 4Q26 with modest N2 ramp dilution 
We expect TSMC’s 3Q26E revenue to grow 15.3% QoQ, followed 
by a further 11.0% QoQ increase in 4Q26E (both in USD terms), 
supported by continued strength in AI/HPC demand. On 
profitability, we model GM edging down to 67.5%/67.3% in 
3Q26E/4Q26E, vs. 67.7% in 2Q26, reflecting dilution from the 
ongoing N2 ramp.  
Momentum sustaining into 2027, supported by broader AI demand 
We expect TSMC’s revenue to grow 36.9% YoY (vs. 32.0% previously) 
in USD terms in 2027E, supported by sustained demand for AI 
accelerators and networking, alongside an increasingly strong 
contribution from server CPUs. At our Communacopia + Technology 
conference, management highlighted that lower token costs should 
stimulate greater AI consumption, while discussions with customers 
and US CSPs has reinforced confidence in demand and 
infrastructure readiness. We believe these trends support continued 
strength in leading-edge demand, particularly for both N2 and N3, 
where management expects supply to remain insufficient despite 
further expansion.

### Page 3

On the supply side, we expect capacity additions, N5-to-N3 conversion, and 
productivity gains to support higher wafer output, while continued CoWoS expansion 
helps enable associated wafer shipments. We model GM improving modestly to 67.5% in 
2027E (vs. 67.2% in 2026E), supported by higher pricing, favorable product mix, 
sustained high utilization and continued productivity gains. 
Higher CapEx to support longer-term expansion 
We maintain our 2026E CapEx forecast at US$64bn, while raising our 2027E/2028E 
forecasts to US$85bn/US$98bn (vs. US$78bn/US$82bn previously), to incorporate 1) 
cost inflation from equipment vendors, and 2) initial spending associated with the 
potential new Texas expansion; however, we expect the new site to only enter mass 
production beyond 2032. For capacity, we continue to model our end-2027E/2028E 
N3/N2 capacity at 200kwpm/140kwpm and 220kwpm/200kwpm, respectively. 
Other key areas in focus for the upcoming analyst meeting 
Long-term AI growth outlook: We believe the broadening of AI demand across 
accelerators, CPUs and networking further supports TSMC’s long-term growth 
opportunity. We would look for updated comments from management on the key 
assumptions underpinning its AI outlook through 2030, where demand drivers have 
strengthened most over the past year, and the factors that could materially change this 
outlook. 
Longer-term CapEx outlook and US expansion: With customer demand continuing to 
drive investment in leading-edge capacity and advanced packaging, we would look for 
management to provide further direction on its longer-term CapEx trajectory and the 
demand visibility supporting these investment decisions. We would also welcome 
updated comments on whether customer demand could lead to further expansion in the 
US beyond current Arizona site, and the potential timing and scale of such investments. 
Foundry competition and technology leadership: We would look for management’s 
perspective on the evolving competitive landscape, including Intel, Samsung and 
Terafab, and the implications for TSMC’s longer-term positioning. In particular, we would 
welcome further discussion of its technology leadership, and whether management 
expects this gap to widen or narrow across upcoming process generations.

Earnings estimate revisions, valuation and risks

Forecast changes 
We have revised up 2026/27/28E earnings by 1%/7%/8% to reflect 1) higher GM 
assumptions as we see a milder GM dilution from N2 ramp, and 2) higher UTRs especially 
for N2/N3 given stronger demand from AI/HPC.

Reiterate Buy; TP raise to NT$3,300 from NT$3,100 
We raise our 12m TP to NT$3,300 from NT$3,100 (2330.TW), following our upward 
earnings revisions. Our TP is based on a target P/E of 22x (unchanged, benchmarked to 
1.0stdv above 5-year avg. forward P/E) applied to our higher 2027E EPS. Accordingly, we 
raise our 12 month TP for the ADR to US$660 from US$620, based on an unchanged FX

### Page 7

Investment Thesis - TSMC (2330.TW/TSM) 
TSMC is a leading global foundry company specializing in cutting-edge nodes. Its leading 
technology stance enables it to enjoy more than 60% revenue share in the global 
foundry market. We like TSMC as we believe its solid technology leadership and 
execution better position it vs. peers to capture the industry’s long-term structural 
growth opportunities, particularly in areas such as AI/5G/HPC/EV. In addition, valuation 
looks attractive in our view, with the shares trading at the mid-range of their 10-year 
trading history (P/E: 10-29x). Furthermore, we view TSMC as the key AI enabler among 
our Taiwan Semis coverage, thanks to its leadership stance in leading edge nodes and 
advanced packaging technology – CoWoS (chip on wafer on substrate). We believe TSMC 
will achieve its 25% revenue CAGR target for the next several years, driven primarily by 
increasing silicon content growth and AI/HPC demand, with LT GM to remain at 56%+. 
We are Buy rated.

Price Target Risks and Methodology - TSMC (2330.TW/TSM) 
Valuation methodology: We have a 12m TP of NT$3,300, which is derived by applying a 
target P/E multiple of 22x to our 2027E EPS. Our 22x target P/E is benchmarked against 
1.0stdv above its 5-year trading average. For the ADR (TSM), we have a 12m TP of 
US$660, based on a USD/TWD rate of 30.0 and an ADR premium of 20%. 
Key downside risks to our views: (1) further deterioration in end-demand recovery 
impacting capacity utilization; (2) slower customer node migrations; (3) slowdown in AI 
investment resulting in lower long-term semiconductor content growth; (4) poor 
yields/execution resulting in worse-than-expected profitability; (5) stronger 
competition resulting in ASP/profitability erosion; and (6) unfavorable FX trend or 
higher-than-expected cost increase weighing on the margin outlook.

Document 099: 8月海外资金重返美债市场，英国持仓首破万亿，中日继续减持、中国持债08年来新低

Source PDF: all report pdf/8月海外资金重返美债市场，英国持仓首破万亿，中日继续减持、中国持债08年来新低.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

据美财政部报告， 8 ⽉海外投资者净买⼊美债 1089 亿美元，持债规模扭转两⽉连降；⼗⼤
持债国家地区中， 8 ⽉只有中⽇持仓下滑，头号 “ 债主 ” ⽇本在⼲预汇市当⽉持仓创 22 个⽉新
低、连降四个⽉，中国持仓连降三个⽉，⼀年来减少 1090 亿美元，第⼆⼤ “ 债主 ” 英国持仓
两⽉连创历史新⾼，加拿⼤ 8 ⽉增持居⾸、增幅 383 亿美元，总持仓排第五，增幅第⼆位⽐
利时增 198 亿美元。
摘要：
美国官⽅数据显⽰，今年 8 ⽉，海外资⾦重返美国国债市场，但主要海外持有国家地区的配置⽅
向仍然分化，前三⼤ “ 债主 ” 中，⽇本和中国⼤陆继续减持，英国持仓续创新⾼。
美国财政部于美东时间 16 ⽇周五公布国际资本流动报告（ TIC ），披露 8 ⽉海外投资者净买⼊美国
国债 1089 亿美元，扭转 7 ⽉净卖出 36 亿美元的抛售势头。按持仓余额统计， 8 ⽉海外投资者持有
的美国国债总规模环⽐增加 833 亿美元，升⾄ 9.3317 万亿美元，在两个⽉连降后反弹。
美债的主要持有国家地区中，英国持仓规模史上⾸次突破 1 万亿美元，加拿⼤、⽐利时也⼤幅增

持。但海外资⾦重返美债市场，并不意味着所有主要持有国都在增加配置。⽇本的持仓⾃ 5 ⽉以
来持续下滑，和⽇本政府抛售美元资产买⼊⽇元的⼲预⾏动期重合，中国⼤陆则延续了⻓期减
持的趋势，⼀年来减少上千亿美元。
⽇本汇市⼲预规模创纪录，美元资产配置受关注
美国财政部 TIC 数据显⽰， 8 ⽉⽇本持有的美国国债规模环⽐ 7 ⽉减少 356 亿美元，降⾄ 1.0683 万
亿美元，仍是美国最⼤的海外 “ 债主 ” 。
据 TIC 报告，⽇本的美债持仓已连续四个⽉下降。此前 6 ⽉、 7 ⽉持仓连续创 2025 年 1 ⽉以来新
低， 8 ⽉进⼀步降⾄ 2024 年 12 ⽉以来最低⽔平。
⽇本持仓持续缩⽔之际，⽇本政府今年多次⼲预外汇市场、试图⽀撑⽇元，市场因此关注汇市
⼲预是否影响⽇本的美元资产配置。
⽇本财务省 5 ⽉ 29 ⽇公布的数据显⽰， 4 ⽉ 28 ⽇⾄ 5 ⽉ 27 ⽇期间，⽇本外汇市场⼲预总额达到
11.73 万亿⽇元。分析⼈⼠认为，⽇本买⼊⽇元、卖出美元的交易可能涉及⼀定规模的美国国债

出售。
此后，⽇美两国还罕⻅地联⼿⼲预汇市。⽇本财务省 8 ⽉ 3 ⽇证实，⽇本政府与美国财政部于 7 ⽉
31 ⽇协调实施了买⼊⽇元的汇率⼲预，以遏制⽇元汇率近期的剧烈波动。这是⽇美两国⾃ 2011
年东⽇本⼤地震后⾸次协调⼲预汇市，也是⾦融危机等特殊时期之外极为罕⻅的联合⾏动。
⽇本财务省随后公布的数据显⽰， 7 ⽉ 30 ⽇⾄ 8 ⽉ 26 ⽇期间，⽇本政府共计投⼊约 15.4 万亿⽇元
⼲预外汇市场，创下单⽉⼲预⾦额新⾼。
⼲预汇市通常需要卖出外汇、买⼊⽇元。由于⽇本外汇储备中持有⼤量美国国债，市场因此关
注，⽇本是否会通过出售美债等美元资产为⼲预⾏动提供资⾦。
不过， TIC 数据并不能直接证明⽇本为⼲预汇市出售了多少美债。持仓余额的变化既受到实际买
卖影响，也可能受到债券价格变化等估值因素影响，不能将⽇本 8 ⽉持仓减少 356 亿美元全部归
因于汇市⼲预。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

中国持仓连降三个⽉  两⽉连创⼗⼋年新低
据 TIC 报告，中国⼤陆 8 ⽉持有的美国国债规模环⽐减少 273 亿美元，降⾄ 5907 亿美元，连续两
个⽉创 2008 年 8 ⽉以来新低，继续位居美国第三⼤海外债主。
截⾄ 8 ⽉，中国⼤陆的美债持仓已连续三个⽉下降，最近 12 个⽉内，有⼗个⽉持仓环⽐下滑。以
TIC ⼀年来的数据计算，截⾄ 8 ⽉末，中国⼤陆所持美国国债相⽐去年同期⼤幅减少 1090 亿美
元。
从更⻓周期看，中国⼤陆持有的美国国债规模已较过去明显下降。 2016 年前后，中国⼤陆持仓
曾超过 1 万亿美元，如今已降⾄约 5900 亿美元，反映出官⽅持仓持续收缩的趋势。
与此同时，市场近期也出现关于中国可能通过第三地持有部分美国债券的讨论。据本周三的报
道，华尔街部分投资者和分析⼈⼠怀疑，中国可能通过加拿⼤等地的⾦融机构持有部分美国债
券。相关讨论与加拿⼤近年来美国国债及相关机构债券持仓增加有关。
但这⼀说法尚⽆确凿证据。加拿⼤持仓上升并不能直接证明其中包含中国转移的资产，也可能

受到其他投资者配置变化的影响。
因此，中国⼤陆持仓持续下滑是 TIC 数据能够确认的趋势，但仅凭公开持仓数据，尚不能断⾔中
国实际持有的全部美国债券规模同步下降。
加拿⼤增持居⾸，⽐利时持仓也⼤增
在 TIC 报告的⼗⼤海外持有美债国家地区中， 8 ⽉只有⽇本和中国的持仓减少。总持仓规模介于
中⽇之间的英国连续两个⽉增持。
数据显⽰，英国持有的美债规模环⽐增加 112 亿美元，升⾄ 1.0095 万亿美元，有纪录以来⾸次突
破 1 万亿美元，连续两个⽉持仓创最⾼纪录，继续稳居 TIC 报告的海外 “ 债主 ” 榜单次席。
加拿⼤和⽐利时的增持幅度更为突出。加拿⼤ 8 ⽉美债持仓环⽐增加 383 亿美元，升⾄约 4646 亿
美元，增幅在美国⼗⼤海外持有国中居⾸，总持仓排名第五。
⽐利时持仓环⽐增加 198 亿美元，升⾄约 4905 亿美元，增幅仅次于加拿⼤，总持仓排名第四。
值得注意的是，市场近期也在讨论第三地托管数据是否完整反映最终投资者的⾝份。美国财政
部的 TIC 数据主要依据托管机构和交易商报告编制，如果外国投资者通过第三国托管机构持有美

国证券，相关资产可能被记在托管地名下，⽽⾮最终所有者所在国家。
因此，单个国家持仓增加并不⼀定完全对应其本国投资者的实际买⼊。尤其在分析中国等⼤型
持有国的美债配置变化时，托管地与最终所有者之间的差异值得留意。
海外私⼈资⾦流⼊占主导，美债需求能否延续
整体来看，中⽇以外的主要持有美债地区 8 ⽉有不同程度的增加。
在美国⼗⼤海外美债持有国家地区中，总持仓第六位的开曼群岛持债⼩幅增加 29 亿美元⾄ 4630
亿美元，第七位的卢森堡持仓增 61 亿美元⾄ 4482 亿美元，第⼋位的法国持仓增加 150 亿美元、
增幅仅次于⽐利时，增⾄ 3634 亿美元，第九位的爱尔兰持仓增 19 亿美元⾄ 3521 亿美元，中国台
湾地区持仓增 23 亿美元⾄ 2984 亿美元，位居第⼗。

TIC 报告还显⽰， 8 ⽉海外投资者整体净买⼊⻓期美国证券 3007 亿美元，其中私⼈投资者净买⼊
2578 亿美元，外国官⽅机构净买⼊ 429 亿美元。若进⼀步计⼊短期证券、银⾏资⾦流动等项⽬，
当⽉美国录得 1998 亿美元的净国际资本流⼊。
这些数据表明， 8 ⽉海外资⾦整体仍在流⼊美国⾦融市场，美债需求也有所恢复。不过，海外资
⾦回流与主要官⽅持有国减持并不⽭盾：不同投资者的配置策略可能不同，私⼈投资者的买⼊
也可以抵消部分官⽅机构的减持。
对美债市场⽽⾔，后续值得关注的，⼀⽅⾯是海外投资者净买⼊能否持续，另⼀⽅⾯是⽇本汇
市⼲预是否继续影响其美元资产配置，以及中国⼤陆官⽅持仓下降在多⼤程度上反映实际减
持。
海外资⾦回流为美债需求提供了⽀撑，但⽇本、中国继续减持，也意味着不能仅凭单⽉总持仓

回升，就认定所有主要海外持有者对美国国债的配置意愿都已同步改善。

美国:ISM制造业PMI 日本:制造业PMI
按美元计，9月我国进口同比增长31%，前值增28.2%；出口同比增长36.3%，前值增25%。9月份贸易顺差
1335.32亿美元，前值1190.85亿美元。9月进出口同比增速较上月有所抬升，继续保持较快增长。出口方面，
产品结构持续优化。从主要出口市场看，9月我国前三大出口市场分别是东南亚联盟、美国和欧盟，其中，
对东盟出口同比增长52.26%，对欧盟出口增长39.56%，对美国出口增长25.25%，对美国、欧盟出口大幅改

善，对东盟及“一带一路”沿线国家出口保持高速增长。从产品结构看，出口产品持续向智向绿方向转型，
机电产品特别是集成电路同比增长显著。进口方面，随着高质量共建“一带一路”扎实推进，我国与全球
贸易伙伴的经贸合作持续深化，优质产品持续进入我国市场。从进口产品结构看，金属矿砂等大宗商品进
口同比增速持续提升；自动数据处理设备及其零部件等机电产品同比增长显著，或能说明高新产业的高出
口需求支撑了进口的景气。展望未来，近期积极因素有所积累，但美元强势带来的去杠杆效应能否缓解，
仍取决于地缘局势能否明朗。具体来看，出口方面重点关注可能增加的贸易壁垒。进口方面，经济政策的
回归或仍需和外部环境相协调，关注后续国内相应政策的落地。

Document 129: [207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 신규 공장이 채운 실적, 수주가 더할 모멘텀삼성바이오로직스(207940) 신규 공장이 채운 실적, 수주가 더_21961458

Source PDF: all report pdf/[207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 신규 공장이 채운 실적, 수주가 더할 모멘텀삼성바이오로직스(207940) 신규 공장이 채운 실적, 수주가 더_21961458.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
미국 록빌·5공장 가세로 3분기 기대치 부합 전망 
삼성바이오로직스의 2026년 3분기 연결 매출액은 1조 3,637억 원(+8.4% Y oY), 
영업이익은 6,092억 원(-3.8% YoY, 영업이익률 44.7%)으로 시장 기대치에 부합할 
전망이다. 5월 파업에 따른 생산 차질에도 1~4공장(총 60.4만 리터)의 풀가동과 
신규 공장의 매출 기여가 외형 성장을 뒷받침할 것으로 예상한다. 미국 록빌 
공장(6만 리터)은 3분기부터 매출 기여가 본격화되고, 5공장(18만 리터)도 
인증용(PPQ) 배치 생산을 통해 매출이 확대될 전망이다. 2분기에는 미국 공장·5공장 
관련 비용이 선반영됐으나, 3분기에는 매출 증가에 따른 고정비 부담 완화로 
영업이익률이 전분기 대비 0.3%p 상승할 것으로 예상한다. 
항체 중심 CDMO에서 펩타이드로 확장 
삼성바이오로직스의 2026년 신규 수주는 약 1.2조 원으로 전년 수주액의 약 20% 
수준에 그치고 있으나, 연말부터 내년까지 수주 모멘텀은 점차 강화될 것으로 
판단한다. 2025년 하반기 이후 미국의 의약품 관세 불확실성이 완화되며 글로벌 
제약사와의 논의가 늘어나고 있어, 신규 수주 계약으로 이어질 것으로 기대된다. 
11월 유상증자 납입이 마무리되면 6공장(18만 리터) 착공과 스위스 폴리펩타이드 
인수가 본격화되며 중장기 성장 기대감도 높아질 전망이다. 특히 폴리펩타이드 
인수가 연내 완료되면 2027년부터 연결 실적에 반영되는 동시에, 항체 의약품 
중심의 CDMO 사업을 GLP-1 등 펩타이드로 확장하는 계기가 될 전망이다. 
목표주가 200만 원으로 하향, 수주 회복이 관건 
삼성바이오로직스의 목표주가를 209만 원에서 200만 원으로 하향한다. 유상증자 
신주 발행에 따른 주당가치 4.7% 희석 효과를 반영했다. 폴리펩타이드 인수 효과는 
거래완료 이후 실적 추정치에 반영할 예정이다. 노조 파업 불확실성이 주가에 
부담으로 작용할 수 있어 신규 수주 모멘텀의 회복이 필요한 시점이다.

Document 140: [402340] SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대하이닉스 자사주 소각_31fbe439

Source PDF: all report pdf/[402340] SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대하이닉스 자사주 소각_31fbe439.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-30
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

SK 스퀘어 (402340/KS) 
하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대 
예정보다 빠른 SK 하이닉스 자사주 매입 
SK스퀘어의 자회사인 SK하이닉스는 지난 8월 20일부터 11월 19일까지 자사주 40
조원(2,407만주, 3.3%)을 매입 예정이다. 9월 29일까지 1,715만주, 예정 매입수량 
대비 71.3%를 매입 완료하였다. 매입 초기 매일 65만주 매입하다가 9월 10일부터는 
60만주를 매입하고 있는데, 이러한 추세가 유지된다면 10월 중순경 자사주 매입이 마
무리될 예정이다. 자사주 매입 후 소각 예정으로 소각이 완료되면 SK스퀘어의 SK하아
닉스에 대한 지분율은 20.0%에서 20.7%로 상승하게 된다.

자사주 매입 이후 추가적인 주주환원에 대한 기대 
SK하이닉스는 지난 8월 19일 정책기간(‘25년~’27년) 동안 발생하는 누적 FCF의 
50% 이상을 주주환원 하는 주주환원 정책을 발표한 바 있다. 26년 SK하이닉스의 예
상 FCF는 164조원(컨센서스 기준)으로 50%를 주주환원 할 경우 자사주 매입 금액
(40 조원)을 제외한 주주환원 재원은 42 조원이다. 잔여 주주환원 재원이 모두 배당으
로 지급된다면 SK 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후 SK 스퀘어의 지분율은 20.7%이므로 
SK스퀘어의 배당수입도 8.7조원에 달한다. 지난 3월 SK스퀘어는 ‘26년~’28년 경
상배당수입의 30% 이상을 주주에게 환원하는 정책을 발표했다. 보수적으로 30%만 
가정하더라도 SK스퀘어의 배당재원은 2.6조원(현 시가총액 대비 1.8%)이다. SK스퀘
어는 연초 주주총회에서 배당가능이익 확보 차원에서 자본준비금 5 조 8,900억원을 
이익잉여금으로 전입한 바 있다. 이는 26년 재무제표가 확정되는 27년 3월 이후 배당
가능이익으로 편입되므로 SK스퀘어의 주주환원 확대가 기대된다.

투자의견 매수, 목표주가 185 만원(유지) 
SK스퀘어의 순자산 가치 중 98% 이상이 SK하이닉스 지분가치이다. SK하이닉스는 
자사주 매입 이후 17.7% 주가가 상승했으나, SK 스퀘어 주가는 11.4% 상승했다. SK
하이닉스 자사주 매입·소각은 SK 스퀘어의 보유 지분가치 상승뿐 아니라 지분율도 상
승하기 때문에 자사주가 소각될 경우 SK스퀘어의 주가가 탄력적으로 움직일 가능성이 
높다. SK스퀘어 현재 NAV 대비 할인율은 43.7%, 12MF PBR 1.4배로 7월 이후 주
가 조정으로 밸류에이션 매력도 높아졌다. 투자의견 매수와 목표주가 185만원을 유지
한다.

2026-09-30

지주회사/통신서비스/ESG. 최관순 / ks1.choi@sks.co.kr / 3773-8812

Document 143: Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
History tends to repeat itself in advanced economies. Once again, growth ultimately fell short of expectations by only a small
margin in the first half of 2026, despite the conflict in Iran. As early as 2025, the impact of tariffs was less severe than feared.
This is a sign that structural factors (notably AI, defence and electrification) are underpinning growth in these countries

and are expected to continue to do so in 2027. However, there are significant risks to the economic outlook, primarily of a
geopolitical nature, with a significant upside risk to inflation and interest rates. The other instance of history repeating itself
concerns a period that those under 20 cannot have experienced: the period leading up to the 2008 crisis, echoes of which can
be seen in terms of growth, inflation, the direction of monetary policy, and long-term interest rates.

Advanced economies are experiencing trends similar to those they
faced nearly 20 years ago:
- Growth remains resilient in 2025-26 despite shocks, as it did in 2006-
07, in the major advanced economies.
- Inflation is above central banks’ targets: as in 2006-07, this calls for a
tightening of monetary policy.
- In Europe and the United States, long-term interest rates have re -
turned to their 2006-07 levels (1996 in Japan): a challenging environ-

ment for public finances in a context where public debt ratios are signi-
ficantly higher than those seen 20 years ago.
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY IN THE MIDST OF A GROWTH CYCLE
We are already well into 2026, and the picture so far is clear: glo -
bal growth is proving resilient. Following a US tariff shock last year,
growth is once again proving, for the time being, to be significantly less
affected by the conflict in Iran than had been anticipated when it first

broke out. Initially, however, there were fears that this new shock would
hit growth even harder. As with the tariff hike in 2025, the impact was
measurable, particularly in terms of consumer confidence. But in both
2025 and 2026, growth has also shown a degree of resilience.
The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise
in oil prices, the recessionary impact of which is not yet apparent.
According to our forecasts, growth was expected to reach or exceed 1%

in 2026 in the four major advanced economies (the Eurozone, Japan,
the United Kingdom and the United States, where it exceeds 2%) for
the second consecutive year. This had not happened (excluding the
post-COVID catch-up period) since 2006-07. Growth figures are more
moderate (particularly for demographic reasons), but the private sec -
tor is not (at present) showing the same excesses (at that time, the
credit boom had preceded the worst crisis since the 1930s); although

debt levels have recently risen in the US tech sector.
The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial
intelligence (AI). It is fuelling global trade and business investment
in most countries. This is clearly the case in the United States, where
business investment is growing strongly as investment plans in the
US tech sector are implemented.
However, the world’s major economies are currently engaged in a
broader push to expand their capital goods investment. Alongside the

ongoing development of services—particularly digital services—we
have seen a rebound in industrial production in most countries since
early 2025. The leading sectors are primarily technology equipment,
machinery, aeronautics, defence and their essential inputs (energy and
metals). This trend is evident everywhere, including in Europe, and
is becoming entrenched over the long term, with an improvement in
business climate indicators, particularly new export orders. Growth is

therefore expected to strengthen further in 2027, particularly in the
Eurozone, where German investment plans are set to gather momen -
tum and whose spillover effects will have a greater impact on the eco-
nomy.
However, not all sectors are experiencing the same momentum. This
is particularly true for the sectors that depend on household consump-
tion, which has shown mixed trends. Our observation that consumer
spending is more buoyant in the United States than in Europe was

confirmed in 2026, and the outlook does not point to a change in this
ranking.
EDITORIAL
RESILIENCE TO SHOCK AFTER SHOCK

### Page 2

EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
While European and US households are experiencing more or less the
same energy price shock, inflation remains higher in the United States.
But this has not prevented consumer spending from keeping pace, pri-
marily because it is driven by the wealthiest households, who are less
sensitive to inflation than to the wealth effect. Furthermore, while the

savings rate remains high in the Eurozone, US households have been
drawing down their savings. This is also true of British households,
which explains why the UK has seen slightly stronger growth since
the beginning of the year. In Japan, government spending has partially
offset energy inflation (while other countries are doing so to a much
lesser extent than in 2022), which has supported consumption growth.
However, these buffers—reduced savings or fiscal support—have their

limits when shocks persist. The two major current conflicts (Iran and
Ukraine) are far from being resolved, and their impact is continuing.
This persistent geopolitical risk and its implications for inflation repre-
sent a significant headwind to the growth outlook for 2027.
HIGHER INFLATION CALLS FOR MONETARY TIGHTENING
Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks
examined in this publication, which is another similarity (albeit with

different underlying causes) with the 2006-07 period. Japan is the ex -
ception, as government support measures for households are tempo -
rarily holding back inflation.
Apart from the Bank of Japan, which is engaged in a long-term norma-
lisation process, no central bank was expected to raise its key rates
in 2026. But they are now being prompted to do so for reasons that
vary from one region to another. The first is the conflict in Iran and
the resulting rise in oil prices, which triggered a rebound in energy

inflation from the second quarter onwards. However, since the conflict
was not resolved by the memorandum of understanding signed in June,
and given that the subsequent drop in oil prices proved short-lived,
inflation is expected to rise again. The rise in gas prices is expected to
exacerbate the impact, particularly in Europe, where inventories are
being replenished ahead of winter. Electricity prices are expected to
follow suit in most countries, but at a more moderate pace than in

2022. Indeed, gas is now used less to generate electricity than it was
at that time (thanks to the expansion of renewable energy), and to an
even lesser extent in France.
There is a significant risk that energy inflation will spread further to
other components of inflation in the future, and inflation expectations
could eventually reflect this. This is why the European Central Bank
has already raised its key interest rate twice (in June and September)

and, according to our scenario, will likely do so again in December. For
the same reason, we expect the Bank of England to raise its policy rate
in November. Although the cap on energy prices was maintained for
a long time, it has now been raised. As a result, inflation is expected
to rise between now and the end of the year. The aim will then be to
prevent second-round effects.
Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength. In the

United States, it has exceeded the 2% target since 2021. The main
argument in favour of a new cycle of rate hikes (following the one in
2022-2023) lies in the current resilience of growth and the improving
labour market. The Federal Reserve (Fed) must balance two equally
important objectives: maximum employment and price stability. Howe-
ver, unlike in the 4 th quarter of 2025, when the decline in job crea -
tion justified monetary easing (by 75 basis points), the current sharp

rebound in job creation completely reverses that analysis. According
to our forecasts, the unemployment rate is expected to decrease in
the coming months, which pushes back the prospect of a decline in
core inflation. This situation persists due to both supply constraints
(over which a central bank has no control) and excess demand (which
the Fed can, however, mitigate). Consequently, the Fed raised its key
rate in September and is expected to implement two further rate hikes

between now and January 2027.
In Japan, finally, while headline inflation has been brought down by
government support measures, core inflation remains significant.
Wage pressures are mounting against a backdrop of labour shortages.
The Bank of Japan is therefore being forced to accelerate the pace of its
monetary normalisation.
In all these regions, inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026
or Q1 2027. It is against this backdrop of temporary inflation that rate

hikes could also come to an end by that time (excluding Japan). Howe-
ver, this assumes that once the energy shock—the course of which
remains uncertain—subsides, inflation will fall. The effects of El Niño,
which are still difficult to gauge at present, could take over. Inflation
would then remain above the central banks’ target for a longer period.
Under this scenario, rate hikes would go further than is currently en -
visaged.
THE RISE IN LONG- TERM INTEREST RATES: A FACTOR THAT WILL WEIGH

ON PUBLIC FINANCES
Monetary policies have also to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal
policy, particularly in the United States (where the budget deficit is
high and shows no signs of shrinking) and in Japan (where the new
government is pursuing pro-growth policies). In Europe, fiscal policy
is playing a much smaller role than it did in 2022. While the stimulus
measures implemented at that time did help reduce the inflation rate
in the short term (by nearly 2.5 pp in France, for example), by keeping

demand at a high level, they delayed disinflation and increased the
scale of the monetary tightening required.
The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: it triggered a
rise in long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the
start of the year) to levels close to those of 2006-07. This rise is wides-
pread, but its causes vary. Inflation expectations appear to play a more
significant role in the Eurozone, whereas the rise in real interest rates

seems to be the main factor in the United States and Japan.

The bank
for a changing
world
Higher inflation and more resilient real growth than feared point in
the same direction: stronger than expected nominal growth that is li -
kely to be sustained. However, in the long term, high nominal growth
tends to drive up nominal interest rates (as it is a positive sign of more
profitable investment projects), in contrast to the problem of secular

stagnation raised in the 2010s (which combined low nominal growth
with low nominal interest rates).
The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period
of growth), with various situations. In several countries, the public
debt-to-GDP ratio continues to rise. In the United States, deficit reduc-
tion does not appear to be a priority, to the extent that it is expected
to remain above 6% of GDP at the very least until the end of Donald

Trump’s term of office (with little hope that the mid-term elections will
significantly alter this trend). In the United Kingdom and France, fiscal
consolidation has begun, but it is being hampered by the simultaneous
increase in certain areas of expenditure (debt servicing, defence and
healthcare, due in particular to an ageing population), which is slowing
the pace of consolidation. In Germany, the implementation of invest -
ment plans is leading to a widening of the deficit. While this does not

pose a sustainability problem, it does result in an increase in annual
debt issuance. In Japan, the budget deficit is kept in check by interest
payments that remain low, as the rise in interest rates is recent and
the average maturity of the debt is high (10 years). However, expansio-
nary fiscal policy could pose further problems in terms of debt dyna -
mics if it were to continue through the end of the decade. In Italy and
Spain, fiscal consolidation has been sufficient to stabilise the public

debt-to-GDP ratio (and even to reduce it in Spain), but the high level of
Italy’s debt service it vulnerable to a rise in interest rates.
In all these countries, the fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it
will be in 2030. Indeed, in most cases, only half of the debt was is -
sued after 2022 (that is, after the end of the zero-interest-rate period).
The debt service has so far only partially risen, except in the United
States, where most of the increase has already occurred due to a shor-

ter average debt maturity (6 years). This means that, for all countries
combined, the rate of GDP growth remains higher than the average
interest rate paid on public debt (or the effective interest rate).
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with
GDP growth. As a result, in most cases, the debt service will have re -
turned to the level as a share of GDP seen in the early 2000s. In order to
stabilise public debt-to-GDP ratios, primary balances will need to have

improved. This will even require the generation of primary surpluses,
particularly in Italy (which is already achieving this), France and the
United Kingdom. This is a crucial shift that governments will need to
introduce as early as the 2027 budget; otherwise, they risk postponing
the stabilisation of their public debt-to-GDP ratio once again.
Article completed on 17 September 2026
Stéphane Colliac
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

Report source: Allianz Trade

### Page 1

In Summary 
• France is the hardest hit in the global bond sell-off, mostly due to its own fiscal and political 
weakness. Since the Hormuz crisis began, 10-year OAT yields have risen 150bps (US +115bps, 
Italy +132bps, Germany +83bps) as the prolonged energy crisis exacerbates fiscal concerns in 
a politically fragile environment. Franc e’ fiscal deficits have remained above 5% of GDP for 
four consecutive years. Debt has risen from 98% of GDP in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, and 
the 2026 deficit is now expected at -5.4% against a -5% initial target. 
• A "short Europe" trade amplifies the move and spreads it to the rest of the Eurozone while 
the ECB looks the other way. Foreign banks and non-banks hold 36% of OATs and Japanese 
investors about 5% (EUR137bn), exposing France to the repatriation trade and price-sensitive 
investors. Speculative shorts, hedge -fund asset -swap unwinds and thin liquidity feed a 
negative loop, w ith market depth more than 50% below normal and the OAT spread over 
Bunds peaking at 150bp s. The speed reflects a positioning shock, not a pure repricing of 
fundamentals. Long -term investors are sitting out ahead of the elections, given budget 
uncertainty and the sensitivity of OATs to political fragility. Spanish and Italian spreads are 
about 20bps wider, bank CDS are widening and French and Italian three -month bills are 
converging toward the ECB's marginal lending rate. Fragmentation is far from 2012 or 2020 
levels, but the ingredients are in place. The ECB has not yet intervened and may limit  itself to 
verbal intervention, while continuing QT and pushing through two more hikes (+50bps).  
• Baseline: A watered -down budget, but the fiscal arithmetic stays heavy for France .  
We expect GDP growth of +0.5% in 2026 and +0.9% in 2027. The Assembly votes on the budget 
on 20 and 27 October, and we expect a budget by year -end through ordinances or Article 
49.3, keeping the 2027 deficit at -5.3% of GDP. This should contain fiscal slippage and stabilize 
financial conditions, with OAT spreads in the 120 -150bps range. Higher rates  (+100bps) will 
feed through over the next three to six months, adding EUR25bn (0.8% of GDP) in gross 
interest expenses for households, non -financial firms and the government combined. The 
debt-stabilizing deficit is -2.7% of GDP, which requires savings of more than  EUR80bn, plus 
EUR15-20bn a year to stop social and interest spending from widening the deficit. This takes 
credible fiscal measures (better tax mix, higher retirement age) and growth -enhancing 
reforms. 
• Adverse scenarios: France-centered crisis or Eurozone crisis? The Middle East situation is far 
from contained; oil prices could stay above USD100/bbl for another six months , leading to a 
further global bond sell -off hitting France the hardest, tightening financial conditions and 
jeopardizing deficit reduction. Domestically, opposition parties could take down the 
government or slippage surprises resurface despite a passed budget, tightening conditions in 
the same way. In this context, GDP would stall at +0.0% in 2027 and spread s reach 150 -
175bps. The ECB would talk up and potentially launch repo intervention. In our view, it would 
take a substantial Eurozone -wide contraction (France -0.5% in 2027 ; Eurozone -0.2%; OAT 
spreads at 175 -200bps) for the ECB to tweak or stop QT, launch open market and repo 
intervention and stabilize funding markets (as OATs and BTPs make up 50% of euro repo 
collateral). 
• Looking ahead, the 2027 elections are the next test, and TPI is a late and uncertain 
backstop. With the presidential election on 18 A pril and 2 May 2027 (legislative elections 
likely to follow), we see four scenarios: i) a President with an absolute majority and allies, who 
delivers fiscal consolidation; ii) a President short of a majority who seeks alliances  (our 
baseline, with the 2028 deficit at -4.9% and spreads above 100bps) ; iii) a President short of a 
majority without alliances  and iv) a political and fiscal crisis, for instance from unfunded tax 
cuts or spending hikes, with spreads above 200bps and widespread contagion. Only in t his 
last scenario would the ECB activate the Transmission Protection Instrument (TPI). The risk is 
a lack of political will rather than of instruments, and redenomination risk is the tail case.

Allianz Research | 9 October 2026 
Toasted, not burnt: France’s bond sell-off  
and the road to the 2027 election

### Page 2

France is the country most affected in the global bond sell-off

Since the start of the Hormuz crisis,  yields on 10 -year French government bonds (OAT) have risen by 150bps  
(vs 115bps for the US, 132bps for Italy and 83bps for Germany), the highest jump among developed economies. 
The feedback loop between rising oil prices and interest rate expectations  has met structurally vulnerable public 
finances whose credibility has been increasingly undermined by growing political fragility since the 2024 dissolution 
of the National Assembly. France’s debt-to-GDP ratio has surged from 98% in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, with 
over 121% of GDP expected in 2027 (vs 83% to 87% in the Eurozone). Meanwhile, the  weakening economy (+0.5% 
GDP growth expected this year)  is leading to a fourth consecutive  year of fiscal slippage : The government now 
expects a deficit of -5.4% of GDP against the -5.1% targeted in the 2026 Budget. With Moody's upcoming review on 
23 October, France is now at risk of losing its AA rating (currently Aa3 by Moody’s vs A+ by S&P and Fitch Ratings, 
i.e. already one notch below); current pricing, if sustained, would even imply a BBB rating.

Table 1: Macroeconomic and fiscal forecasts for France in 2027

Government Allianz Research 
Real GDP growth (%) 1.0 0.9 
Inflation (CPI. %) 1.8 2.2 
Public balance (% GDP) -5.0 -5.3 
Primary balance (% GDP) -2.1 -2.5 
Debt service costs (% GDP) 2.9 2.8 
Public debt (% GDP) 121.7 121.8

Sources: French government, Allianz Research

France is the first victim of the Japanese repatriation trade.  More than other European sovereigns, France's 
investor base is exposed to price -sensitive players. The most volatile group, foreign banks and non -banks, holds 
36% of French government bonds. Domestic banks and non-banks, whose holdings fluctuate less, account for 32%, 
while non -price sensitive investors (domestic and foreign central banks) make up only 32%. Other European 
countries have either a stronger anchor in the domestic market (Italy  with 66% vs 47% for France ) or with foreign 
central banks ( Germany with 26% vs 16 % for France as Bunds as the primary FX reserve vehicle). Among foreign 
holders, Japanese investors play a significant role , holding around 5% of outstanding French government bonds 
(~EUR137bn). As interest rates rise in Japan, these positions become less attractive relative to  domestic holdings. 
At best they are no longer rolled over, at worst they are actively liquidated. In the recent bond sell -off France 
became the first victim of the J apanese repatriation trade, even though it plays only a minor role compared with 
the US as an overweight in Japanese investors' benchmark allocations .

Figure 1: France’s risk premium is very sensitive to changes in political fragility

### Page 3

Fiscal concerns, political fragility and investor base have created the vulnerability ; sentiment and positioning 
determine the market reaction. A "short Europe" trade has built up for some time in markets and France is identified 
as the most vulnerable link (Figure 2). A negative feedback loop is now at work. A bearish narrative around political 
fragility and fiscal risk is amplified by very large speculative short positions, sales from Japanese institutional 
investors, hedge fund unwinding asset swap positions and a l imited presence of buyers, with market depth more 
than 50% below normal. In such conditions , even small trades can move prices sharply. OAT yields have spiked 
rapidly, with the 10-year spread vs the Bund widening to a peak 150bps, rising twice as fast as during the Eurozone 
crisis. Realized OAT spread volatility has jumped to the 90th percentile. These market dynamics point to massive 
repositioning rather than a repricing of fundamentals . But as long as a stabilizing force of buyers is missing, this 
volatility can persist.

Figure 2: A Short Europe trade has built up with pressure on EGB spreads and EUR -USD

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

As c ontagion has switched side s, the European Government Bond (EGB) market could move from country -
specific fragility to general fragmentation. The OAT sell-off has already spilled over to other EGBs. Unlike 15 years 
ago, when France fell victim to contagion from the periphery, it is now the other way round. Spanish and Italian 
bond yields rose significantly, while German yields fell. Their 10-year spreads versus the Bund widened by around 
20bps. This decoupling within the EGB space is accompanied by widening bank CDS spreads, especially in France. 
Meanwhile, Euro funding market is under elevated stress. French and Italian three-month bills are converging 
toward the ECB's Marginal Lending Facility rate, the effective ceiling for Eurozone money-market rates. A break 
above the MLF would signal a disruption in funding  markets. The ingredients for a broader fragmentation of 
monetary conditions are thus in place, even if the current magnitude  remains far from those of  2012 or 2020. 
However, monetary transmission is also a function  of time: If these stretched conditions persist for an extended 
period, an intervention of the ECB will be more likely , e specially if we see a persistent decoupling of yield 
movements between core and periphery that can feed fears of redenomination risk.

What’s next?

The 2027 budget bill is facing stiff opposition at the National Assembly, putting the planned deficit reduction in 
jeopardy. Presented to the cabinet on 1 October, the draft budget (PLF) and the social security finance bill (PLFSS) 
for 2027 target a public deficit of -5.0% of GDP, down from -5.4% in 2026. The budget is essentially about preventing 
the deficit from getting worse rather than a fiscal adjustment. The government has announced  EUR43bn of new 
fiscal-measures (1.4% of GDP) , split between EUR25bn (58%) in spending restraint (including slowing spending 
compared to expected trends such as healthcare spending, central government cuts, social spending restraint, local 
authorities cuts) and EUR18bn (42%) in new revenues.

First, opposition parties are already pushing back against most of the big revenue raisers measures, including the

### Page 4

cut to the tax allowance on pensioners’ income (EUR1.4bn), the taxation of sick leave (0.5bn) and cutbacks on 
funding to local governments (EUR2.5bn). Second, the persistence of elevated energy prices and tighter financial 
conditions could pressure GDP growth next year, denting expected government receipts. The government expects 
GDP growth at +1% in 2027, while we e xpect +0.9% but with increasing downside risks. The next key milestones to 
watch will be 20th and 27 October when the National Assembly votes on the Draft budget part I (revenues) and 
the Social Security finance bill (Figure 3). The debt stabilizing fiscal deficit stands at -2.7% of GDP, half of today’s 
level, which entails an improvement of the public deficit of EUR83bn from 2027 on top of around EUR15-20bn of 
additional savings to find each year  just to prevent the deficit from rising  (to contain rising social security and 
interest spending).

Figure 3: France’s key political & budget milestones

Note: legislative elections are not yet scheduled – the National Assembly’s term runs to 2029 unless the next president dissolves 
it. Sources: LOLF art. 45, Constitution art. 47, Assemblée nationale budget calendar of 15 Jul 2026, rating agencies, Allianz  
Research

We lay out different scenarios of what could happen between now and the elections (Figure 4). Starting in the 
short-term, we envisage three broad possibilities: i) a watered-down budget passed before year-end which plans a 
stabilization or a slight improvement of the deficit, ii) a political crisis  with opposition parties taking down the 
government and iii) a re-intensification of the energy crisis.

Figure 4: Economic, political and market scenarios October 2026-April 2027

Source: Allianz Research 
In our baseline scenario, we  expect the French government will manage to pass a watered-down budget while 
the energy crisis eases,  allowing the deficit to remain in check in 2027 (-5.3% of GDP) Opposition parties do not

### Page 5

have an incentive  to soften their stance with the Presidential elections approaching on 18 April and 2 May 2027. 
But they also do not have an incentive to create a political crisis by taking down the government. Notably, the 
National Rally (Rassemblement National, RN) has expressed a preference for implementing a budget rather than 
relying on the ‘’Special Law’’ mechanism that was used in early 2025 and early 2026 In this environment, we expect 
the government to manage to pass a mini budget, by year -end, of limited fiscal effort just to keep the deficit in 
check (at -5.3% of GDP), either through executive ordinances – a budgetary mechanism never previously utilized 
under the Fifth Republic – or by triggering Article 49.3.  
In this baseline scenario, OAT spreads should enter a phase of stabilization but contagion risk remains. After the 
clean-up of derivative positions, we see 10 -year OAT spreads trading in a pre -election range of 120 -150bps. But 
the evolution of the French spread will not be isolated , with the potential to trigger a renewed contagion move.  
The French budget and election situation feeds the “short Europe trade”, and this negative sentiment dominates 
country-specific fundamentals. We also do not believe that stabilizing the French situation alone will be enough to 
bring the EGB market back into a convergence trade. Now that dislocations have emerged, EGB spreads are likely 
to become the new playground for speculative (leveraged) players looking to exploit them. With this comes 
elevated rates and FX  volatility and the risk of money market stress. We expect the ECB in this case to limit itself 
first to verbal intervention like a change in wording from "monitoring" to "ready to act" or "disorderly market 
dynamics". We see the tipping point at a 10-year OAT spread above 150bps. 
In a more adverse scenario where the energy crisis does not ease and/or France slips into another political crisis, 
a crisis emerges, either France-centered, or with spillovers and contagion to other Eurozone countries.  Under a 
scenario of renewed tensions in energy markets, the global markets selloff would intensify. Financial conditions 
would tighten more in France, weakening the economy and putting in jeopardy the deficit reduction. Alternatively, 
the government could be taken down in Parliament, triggering a grave political crisis and leading to tighter 
financial conditions. Even under our baseline scenario of a watered-down budget, fiscal slippages could re-emerge 
– for example if the economy is weaker than expected, or if the fiscal adjustment fall s short of what is planned  
(e.g. if revenue -raising measures disappoint). Additionally, under a Special Law, the risk of wider deficit could 
increase if continued through the presidential elections  in 2027, which could lead to France -centered crisis or a 
Eurozone crisis if tightening financial conditions in France spill over to other Eurozone countries . In a France -
centered crisis we would expect the French economy t o enter a mid-sized recession as tighter financial conditions 
would weigh on domestic demand (see next section) , with GDP at +0.0% in 2027. In a Eurozone crisis , we would 
expect French GDP to contract -0.5% and the whole Eurozone economy -0.2%.  
In this adverse scenario, we could see the 10-year OAT spread rising to 1 75-200bps. This would create 
widespread contagion in the EGB market and in the banking sector. In this case, it will clearly be up to the ECB not 
to let the situation escalate into a euro crisi s 2.0. Unlike in the euro crisis, when the escalation was fueled by a lack 
of instruments while the political will was there, this time the deployment of the instruments (TPI, OMT) could fail 
due to a lack of political will and institutional inertia. It wo uld be particularly precarious if markets began to price 
redenomination risk, i.e. the possibility of a country leaving the Eurozone. But we see this scenario as a tail risk. If 
the EGB and money markets move towards general fragmentation, we expect the ECB to set operational steps (QT 
tweak, temporary operations) at OAT spreads around 175bps, but only alongside broader contagion with BTP 
spreads around 150bp s and Spain and Portugal widening together. For France, a QT tweak would represent a 
relief of EUR4.8bn on average per month, equivalent to around 20% of monthly issuances since June. But the 
problem is that “Europe short traders” can also do this math and adjust accordingly. At this point the ECB would 
not only operate in the secondary bond market but a lso need to stabilize funding markets as in  French OAT and 
Italian BTP represent 50% of the EUR repo market collateral. The TPI activation would be late in the process and 
require OAT spread around 200bps with widespread and persistent contagion.

In 2027, the presidential (and likely legislative) elections  could re -escalate economic and market pressures.  
We envisage four scenarios, which we detail more in Figure 5. In a nutshell, under our baseline assumptions, the

### Page 6

newly elected President will not secure a n absolute majority when she or he provokes legislative elections.  
Nevertheless, the President will have the strongest group at the National Assembly and will benefit from strong 
popular support. In this environment, we would expect the President to negotiate with some opposition parties to 
pass a budget putting the deficit on a downward trajectory. Nevertheless, deficit reduction will be limited ( -4.9% 
GDP deficit expected in 2028) amid strong aversion for rapid fiscal tightening in Parliament and public opinion. The 
10-year spread will likely remain above 100bps. In an adverse scenario of political and fiscal crisis, for instance 
triggered by the enactment of tax cuts and/or spending hikes  not credibly funded, a France-centered or Eurozone 
crisis scenario would re-emerge. In the latter case, the ECB would likely intervene with the TPI with French spreads 
breaking 200bps and Italian spreads above 175bps.

Figure 5:  Economic, political and market scenarios after April 2027

Source: Allianz Research

In the short term, a significant easing of global yields and elevated French spreads hinges on three factors:   
1) a substantial easing of the energy crisis, which would reduce inflationary pressures, limiting or even ending 
further central bank hikes. 2) Strong and credible fiscal -consolidation measures passed by decree, though this 
remains unlikely before the presidential election. One option used previously is  reducing healthcare spending, but 
the amount of savings is limited to EUR5-10bn (Table 2). 3) A cooling US economy could moderate the global bond 
sell off, though this too seems less likely before 2027.

Table 2: Measures the French government can implement by decrees to bypass parliamentary vote

Measure Can government act without new parliamentary vote? 
Cancel already authorized State spending Yes 
Freeze/cancel some budgetary appropriations Yes 
Adjust medicine reimbursement within regulatory powers Yes 
Adjust healthcare tariffs/payment parameters Yes, within statutory framework 
Tighten implementation of existing social schemes Often yes 
Change parameters of social contribution rebates  Sometimes yes 
Abolish/reduce a statutory benefit Generally, no 
Permanently change tax rates No 
Fundamentally redesign social-contribution exemptions Generally, requires legislation 
Change the ONDAM target itself Generally, Parliament/LFSS

Sources: French government, various, Allianz Research 
In the longer-term, only a sizeable fiscal -consolidation program can ease French spreads sustainably.  
Ideally, a combination of the following would have the most benefits:

### Page 7

Credible fiscal adjustment (the least growth -hurting). On the revenue side, fiscal consolidation should rely on a 
more efficient tax mix to limit negative growth effects. An increase of  VAT rates would bring large receipts without 
hurting potential growth. Freez ing income-tax thresholds should be preferred to increase social contributions to 
limit the negative impact on labor demand and labor supply amid already high labor taxation in France. Some 
inefficient and costly tax rebates and loopholes could also be cut down. But most of the fiscal adjustment should 
be operated through expenditure cuts. An increase of the effective age of retirement – for instance through a rise 
in the number of years of contribution – would bring substantial savings and increase tax receipts. Substantial 
savings could also be enacted on public agencies and local governments’ administrative costs. In parallel, the 
government could launch a comprehensive spending review to improve efficiency  of public expenditure, 
benchmarking what has been done in the past in countries such as Canada and Sweden.  
Growth-enhancing reforms. The government could implement structural reforms which will likely support growth 
in the medium -term, reducing the need for further painful fiscal adjustment later on. A pension reform would not 
only bring higher receipts and increase savings; it would also stimulate labor supply and potential growth 1. Other 
growth-enhancing measures include a revamp of school education met hods, scaling back excessive regulation, 
incentivizing higher-skilled education and hiring through a reform of the taxation system.

The trickle-down effect of tighter financial conditions

The ongoing bond market problem could increasingly become an economic problem through tighter financial 
and credit conditions, but the economy has buffers to sustain a short -term financial shock. Higher sovereign 
interest rates have started to filter through on to consumer, mortgage and corporate funding costs, and the pa ss-
through will continue through 2027. This will tighten the credit and financial cycle, through higher borrowing costs 
on new lending, higher refinancing bills and tighter access to credit, and ultimately weaken consumer and 
investment spending. Our BIS-built Financial condition index (FCI) for France shows that overall financial conditions 
have tightened sharply recently, but from a relatively loose level (Figure 6, left). They are now close to their long -
term historical averages. Besides, the credit impu lse for households and corporations remain s supportive to date 
(Figure 6, right). During the Eurozone crisis of 2011 -13, the credit impulse turned sharply negative but GDP growth 
held up. While the latest ECB Bank Lending Survey (BLS) points to the credit impulses weakening in the next couple 
of quarters, this does not necessarily mean that GDP growth will weaken sharply. The French economy has buffers 
to withstand a negative finance shock, provided it does not last too long and does not get worse. In parti cular, 
households have high savings and corporates’ cash reserves have been rebuilt recently, though many firms struggle 
with elevated bankruptcies.

rates would almost double households’ net interest income in 2026 to -EUR16.3bn from -EUR8.5bn in 2025, or the 
equivalent of 0.8% of disposable income (see Figure 5). Contrary to US or UK households, French households tend 
to lose when interest rates increase. We calculate that the interest earned on assets would reach EUR24.5bn while 
the interest paid would rise to EUR40.7bn.

Figure 7: Impact of a +100bps shock on interest rates for French households

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

For French non-financial companies, around 50.6% of debt is due to reset or mature within the next 12 months, 
well below the level in Italy, Spain, the UK and the US, where 78 to 90% is due for refinancing. However, France 
starts from the largest corporate debt stock in Europe in this sample, at around EUR2.27trn. This means that even 
with a lower refinancing share, around EUR1.15trn of debt still needs to be repriced within a year, more than in 
Germany, It aly o r Spain. In addition, the pressure is amplified by the starting level of borrowing costs. French 
corporates already face an effective interest rate of 6.1%, the highest among the European peers shown here. Under 
a +100bp s interest-rate shock, the effective pass -through would amount to roughly +89bp s, adding around 
EUR10bn to annual interest expenses. Relative to gross operating surplus, this represents an additional burden of 
about 2.1% of GOS, the highest impact across similar peers, and could shave roughl y -0.7pp off gross margins (see 
Figure 6). For the French government, a +100bps increase in the weighted average interest rates would mean +0.3% 
of GDP in 2027 (EUR 11.7bn) and +0.7% of GDP by 2035. Indeed, France has a rather long average maturity of its 
debt (>8.5 years) which protects it from very high volatility on its interest expenses.

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
This is a joint blog with Alex Etra, Senior Macro
Strategist and head of Flow of Funds at Vanda
Research.
The IMF’s data on the currency composition of
foreign exchange (FX) reserves is scrutinized carefully
for any signs that the world is shifting way from the
dollar.
Yet it doesn’t really matter that much if the dollar’s
share of reserves has shifted from 56.5 percent to 57
percent. The ink-to-impact ratio of the quarterly

reporting on the latest COFER data is all off.
Neither the stock of global reserves nor the stock of
dollar reserves has changed much in the last ten years.
The action is elsewhere.

### Page 3

3
China’s state banks (per the Bank of International
Settlements) have almost as many foreign assets as the
central bank (PBOC).
Japan’s Government Pension Investment Fund
(GPIF) has almost as many foreign assets ($986
billion) as the government has FX reserves ($1.1
trillion at the end of August). Japan’s FX reserves are
on the books of the Ministry of Finance and the
GPIF is overseen by the Ministry of Health, Welfare
and Labor—so these are almost all assets of the

Government of Japan or the broader public sector,
not its central bank.
Korea’s National Pension Service has more foreign
assets than the Bank of Korea has FX reserves.
A different dynamic is in play in Taiwan, as the
government doesn’t run a big retirement fund. But in

### Page 4

4
a world where the hedge ratio of the life insurers has
been (and still is) used as a policy tool, the shift in
Taiwan’s foreign asset accumulation away from the
Central Bank of China (CBC) and over to
institutions whose activities in the foreign currency
market it directly influences broadly fits into the same
theme. So too does the PBOC’s use of the
“macroprudential adjustment factor” effectively adjust
banks’ net foreign assets in much the same way that

CBC regulations adjust lifer hedge ratios.
In the last year, the lifers’ hedge ratios—and their
direct hedges with the central bank—have been one
of the key tools the CBC has used to manage the
Taiwan dollar (and guard the lifers’ solvency).
Asia’s big surplus economies are in a sense just
catching up with the world’s oil exporters. Norway
put its oil surplus into a sovereign wealth fund from
the start. Kuwait, Qatar, and Abu Dhabi (the most
oil-rich emirate) all also have large sovereign funds.

And the world’s biggest oil exporter has joined them:
The Public Investment Fund has almost as many
foreign assets as the Saudi Central Bank (SAMA).*
What’s more, the available evidence suggests that the
dollar share of these pools is in line with or higher
than the dollar share of global FX reserves (it depends
on the institution and jurisdiction).

### Page 5

5
In other words, by looking at the (easily available)
data on the world’s static holdings of formal FX
reserve assets, scholars and analysts miss most of the
growth in the world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign
foreign assets.
Consider China.
No serious analyst now disputes that China’s state
banks—including the policy banks (the China
Development Bank, China Exim)—hold several
trillion in foreign assets.
China reports $3.3 trillion in gross foreign assets to

the BIS (the net position, counting foreign bank
claims on the entire Chinese economy not just the
banks, is $2.5 trillion). That maps to the BOP data,
which shows almost $4 trillion in gross outflows
through the banking system (technically, the sum of
gross outflows in “other” plus the $500 billion in
foreign currency bonds held by the state commercial
banks)

### Page 6

6
Since 2010 (and even more so after 2014) the net
outflow through the state banks has exceeded reserve
accumulation.
The broad contours of this story are confirmed by the
balance sheet data reported by state commercial
banks in their 2025 annual reports, which showed

### Page 7

7
that the top five banks held a combined $2.5 trillion
in foreign currency assets (mostly held abroad).
The Chinese haven’t disclosed the foreign assets of
the two policy banks (with at this stage the complicity
of the IMF, which has neither analyzed the role of
SAFE policy bank financing, nor highlighted the
glaring gap in China’s own reporting). But the work
of AidData points to nearly $1 trillion in foreign
assets, with a hefty dollar share.
The available data sources all suggest that the bulk of

banks—puts the dollar share of their offshore foreign
currency assets at around 70 percent. That is well
above the last disclosed dollar share of China’s formal
reserves (55 percent in 2019). The banks’ annual

### Page 8

8
reports suggest that about 70 percent of the banks’
assets are in dollars and Hong Kong dollars (55
percent USD/15 percent HKD)—a bit below the
dollar and Hong Kong dollar share of their liabilities
(swaps out of the HKD fund a decent share of the
banks euro assets).
Put simply, SAFE’s static dollar holdings aren’t the
important story.
The real story is the rapid growth of the state banks
dollar holdings.
Japan holds a high (though undisclosed) dollar share

in its FX reserves, and those reserves are primarily
invested in Treasuries—so Japan’s MoF is now clearly
the largest contributor to the U.S. data on foreign
official holdings of Treasuries. (SAFE has shifted its
funds out of U.S. custodians, and thus increasingly
appears in the data as a “private” holder in a European
custodial center)
Japan’s formal reserves haven’t changed much in the
last ten years; periodic sales to limit the yen’s weakness
have offset most of the MoF’s accumulated interest

income. Foreign currency reserves were $1.2 trillion
in 2014 and $1.16 trillion at the end of Q1. They
dipped to about $1 trillion at the end of August after
sales of close to $100 billion at the end of July/start of
August.

### Page 9

9
What has grown is the foreign portfolio of the GPIF.
it was around $400 billion in 2014, ~$1 trillion in Q1
of 2026.
GPIF’s foreign bonds aren’t held exclusively in dollars,
but roughly 52 percent of its foreign bonds (26
percent of all bonds) are USD-denominated.
But the GPIF, like most international investors, holds
a large share of its equity portfolio in the U.S. (~$165
billion, 65 percent of the foreign equity portfolio).
Korea is a more extreme version of Japan.

The Bank of Korea’s FX reserves have been roughly
$375-450 billion over the last ten years or so. Around
two-thirds of those are now in dollars (as of the end of
2025).

### Page 10

10
From 2012 to 2025, the foreign assets at the NPS
have grown from ~$80 billion (around 21 percent of
total assets) to over $660 billion (55 percent of total
assets)—and something like 70 percent of those are in
dollars. 76 percent of the fund’s equities are in dollars,
and 66 percent of its debt. And while a lower share of
its “alternative” portfolio is in North America, most
alternative mandates are awarded in dollars.

### Page 11

11
In other words, the majority of the dollar assets held
by Korea’s government (the NPS reports to the
Ministry of Health and Welfare, and the Minister of
Health chairs its asset allocation committee) aren’t
held by the central bank and aren’t in the IMF’s
COFER data set.
Taiwan is an unusual case, as the life insurers aren’t
technically sovereign investors. But with their
regulator now explicitly encouraging the them to hold
an unhedged foreign currency book and hence they

now rely on the state for their solvency, they have a bit
of an official character (a significant appreciation of
the TWD would blow through their volatility reserves
and leave them with fewer assets than liabilities, as
their foreign assets are largely held against TWD
policies). The CBC certainly now uses the lifers desire
to unwind existing hedges to help manage the Taiwan

### Page 12

12
dollar. And they have an exceptionally high dollar
share in their foreign assets—over 95 percent.
The Saudi Public Investment Fund (PIF) is another
example.
It keeps roughly $200 billion of its $250 billion in
foreign investments in dollars—well above the U.S.
share of a standard global stock index. Almost all of
the PIF’s $30 billion in external debt is also in dollars.
And the PIF now has only slightly fewer foreign
assets than the Saudi Central Bank has reserves.**

The broad story is thus pretty clear: the growth in the
world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign assets is not
coming through an increase in FX reserve holdings
managed by the world’s central banks.

### Page 13

13
And, in the critical case of China, the dollar share of
China’s quasi-sovereign foreign assets is likely higher
than the dollar share of their formal FX reserves.
Adam Tooze has noted that inflows into the U.S. are
now coming from investors looking for a profit, not
investors looking for safety. What he calls the “profit
dollar” manifests itself in a number of different ways.
More inflows into corporate bonds and equities for
one. Less official demand for Treasuries for another.

More opacity too.
Those same trends are at play among sovereign and
quasi-sovereign investors.
So don’t obsess about the dollar’s chare in formal FX
reserves. Do recognize that the dollar’s “reserve
currency role” isn’t the source of any significant new
inflows into the dollar.

### Page 14

14
The dollar’s dominant role in the international
monetary system depends on much more than the
size and composition of central bank FX reserves. It is
as much, perhaps more so (given that FX reserve
managers are themselves ultimately liability matchers)
a function of the portfolio choice of a set of
private/semi-private/quasi-sovereign investors that
are much more difficult to observe. Reserve currency
status is not only about FX reserves in a strict sense.

That’s been true for some time. Most of the current
flow into U.S. from the “official” sector is coming
from investors who are not classic reserve managers.
Those flows remain heavily tilted toward the dollar, at
least for now.***
And any real de-dollarization would likely occur first
among these investors.
Put differently, the dollar’s global role is increasingly
as a source of returns, not a source of safety. The
foreign bid, private and public, is for risk, not for

Treasuries. That doesn’t help Scott Bessent much
right now, but it has helped keep valuations in the
stock market extended. And the global debate on the
dollar’s role lags the evolution in the dollar’s role, and
the risks associated with that new role.
It is currently fashionable in some circles to point to
the diminution of the “convenience yield” on

### Page 15

15
Treasuries even while the U.S. dollar continues to
enjoy such a privilege. But the shift in the global
investor base, and their apparent portfolio preference
for U.S. risk assets in lieu of Treasuries, may help
explain the divergence between the convenience yield
on UST and the convenience yield on USD.
And of course, if the negative convenience yield
implies that Treasuries are trading at a historical
discount, the persistence of the latter—and its

concentration in risk assets—suggests that the “profit
dollar” itself is likely trading at a historical premium.
That apparent premium on U.S. risk assets deserves as
much (or more) scrutiny as the currency composition
of central bank FX reserves.
* The PIF and Saudi Aramco also now have a certain
amount of debt.
** Reserves (now mostly in Treasuries, so should
appear in the TIC data, unlike at some times in the
past.

Report source: BNP Paribas

Status: ok

### Page 1

Energy shock: Dashboard 2026 vs. 2022
10/2/2026 2
Will the same causes produce the same effects? In other words, will the war in Iran and the resulting
surge in oil and gas prices lead to an inflationary shock comparable to that seen in 2022? Will their
negative effects on growth be the same as those of the war in Ukraine and the subsequent energy
shock?
We have selected a set of indicators to track the impact of this new energy shock — caused by the war

in the Middle East — on activity and prices in the Eurozone, the United States, oil and gas markets and
emerging countries, and to see how much the current situation resembles that of 2022 at the outbreak
of the conflict in Ukraine.
This dashboard features charts and comments that will be updated on a monthly basis for as long as
necessary.
Overall, based on data available through September 2026, economic activity continues to show
resilience. The high levels of business climate surveys illustrate this well. Household confidence,

however, is more vulnerable and sensitive to energy price pressures. Financing conditions in emerging
economies also continue to hold up well against the shock (much better than in 2022) as well as
against the tensions in DM bond markets, which intensified this summer, partly in the wake of the
reinforcing upward pressure on energy prices. Higher energy prices are also fueling inflationary
pressures and pushing inflation even higher. In particular, Eurozone inflation rose by 0.6 percentage

points to 3.8% year-on-year (September flash estimate), reaching its highest level in three years.

to a 3-year high
